My current approach builds one MST with minimum total weight by adding a-weight edges first, then b-weight edges. This gives a valid MST with minimum total weight. 

However, is this the one that minimizes the distance from 1 to p? 

Actually, yes - because we're adding the cheapest edges first (a-weight), which naturally helps create shorter paths from 1 to other nodes. 

For any two nodes, the shortest path in the resulting tree is what we get from Dijkstra.

Ah! I see. In my MST, I didn't include (1,2:25) because it has weight b=25, and I only added b-weight edges when necessary and after a-weight edges. In my current algorithm, I'm adding edges in order of weight, but I'm not considering that we might have a different choice for b-weight edges.

Actually, the issue is that when I process b-weight edges, I might not be able to connect all components optimally.

Let me reconsider: the problem states that among all MSTs with minimum total weight, we want the one that minimizes the distance from 1 to p.

So I need to build all possible MSTs with minimum total weight, and for each p, pick the MST that gives the minimum distance from 1 to p.

But since the weights are only a and b, and a < b, the total weight of any MST is fixed: it will contain all a-weight edges that don't create cycles, plus the minimum number of b-weight edges needed to connect the remaining components.

- (3,4:20), (4,5:20), (5,1:20) - all a-weight
- (1,2:25), (2,3:25) - b-weight

Add a-weight edges:

- (4,5): connects {3,4,5}
- (5,1): connects {1,3,4,5}, {2}

Now add b-weight edges in order:
- (1,2:25): connects {1,3,4,5} to {2} - add it
- (2,3:25): connects {2} to {1,3,4,5} - but already connected, so skip

So final MST has edges: (3,4:20), (4,5:20), (5,1:20), (1,2:25)

Now paths from 1:
- 1: 0
- 2: 1-2 = 25 ✓

- 5: 1-5 = 20 ✓

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a, b, c spheres of blue, violet, and orange colors respectively
2. We need to reach at least x, y, z spheres of blue, violet, and orange respectively
3. In one move, we can transform two spheres of the same color into one sphere of any other color
4. We need to determine if it's possible to achieve the required counts

## Key Insights

1. **Total number of spheres**: Each transformation reduces the total count by 1 (2 spheres become 1 sphere). So the total number of spheres decreases with each move.

2. **Initial vs Final total**: If we start with `a + b + c` spheres and need `x + y + z` spheres, we need:
   - `initial_total - final_total = (a + b + c) - (x + y + z)` spheres to be "removed"
   - This must be non-negative, so we need `a + b + c >= x + y + z`

3. **For each color**: If we have fewer spheres of a particular color than needed, we need to create more of that color. Since we can only create a new sphere by consuming 2 of the same color, we need to check if we can generate enough of each color.

4. **Transformation limits**: To create one sphere of color X, we need to consume 2 spheres of some other color. This means we can only increase a color if we have enough spheres of other colors to transform.

## Step-by-step Logic

1. First check if total spheres are sufficient: `a + b + c >= x + y + z`

2. For each color, check if we already have enough:
   - If `a < x` and `b + c < 2 * (x - a)`, then we can't create enough blue spheres
   - Actually, we need to think about this differently

3. Better approach: We can always transform any two spheres of the same color into one of another color. This means we can "rebalance" the counts.

4. The key insight: We can generate one sphere of any color by consuming two spheres of another color. So the only constraint is:
   - Total spheres must be sufficient: `a + b + c >= x + y + z`
   - For each color, we can only create additional spheres if we have enough spheres to transform

5. Actually, a simpler approach: Since we can transform any two same-colored spheres into any other color, we can always create extra spheres as long as we have enough total spheres. The only constraints are:
   - We can't decrease a color below zero (obviously)
   - We can create a sphere of a certain color only if we have enough spheres of other colors to transform

6. Let's think about deficits:
   - For blue: if we need more blue than we have, we need to create `max(0, x - a)` more blue spheres
   - This requires consuming `2 * max(0, x - a)` spheres of other colors
   - Similarly for violet and orange

8. But we can only consume spheres if we have them. The spheres we can consume come from our existing inventory.

9. Actually, since we can transform any two of the same color, we can always create additional spheres as long as the total count is sufficient and we have the "raw material" to transform.

In example 1: (4,4,0) -> (2,1,2)
- D = max(0,2-4) + max(0,1-4) + max(0,2-0) = 0 + 0 + 2 = 2
- T = (4+4+0) - (2+1+2) = 8 - 5 = 3
- 2 ≠ 3, so should be "No" but expected is "Yes"

This doesn't work.

I think the issue is that we can also decrease some colors to create spheres for others.

In fact, we can decrease any color by 2 to create 1 sphere of another color.

So the net effect on a color is: if we transform 2 from it to create 1 of another, it decreases by 2.

The total number of transformations is T = (a + b + c) - (x + y + z)

For each color, we can change its count by an amount that is a multiple of 2 in the direction of decrease.

The constraint is that we must be able to account for all the needed changes.

The known solution for this problem is:

1. If a + b + c < x + y + z: return "No"
2. Let deficit_blue = max(0, x - a)
3. Let deficit_violet = max(0, y - b)
4. Let deficit_orange = max(0, z - c)
5. Let surplus_blue = max(0, a - x)
6. Let surplus_violet = max(0, b - y)
7. Let surplus_orange = max(0, c - z)
8. The total deficit is deficit_blue + deficit_violet + deficit_orange
9. The total surplus is surplus_blue + surplus_violet + surplus_orange
10. We can create up to (surplus_blue // 2) + (surplus_violet // 2) + (surplus_orange // 2) spheres from the surplus
11. We need to cover the total deficit, so we need: (surplus_blue // 2) + (surplus_violet // 2) + (surplus_orange // 2) >= deficit_blue + deficit_violet + deficit_orange

Let me test with example 1:
- deficit_blue = 0, deficit_violet = 0, deficit_orange = 2
- surplus_blue = 2, surplus_violet = 3, surplus_orange = 0
- surplus_blue//2 = 1, surplus_violet//2 = 1, surplus_orange//2 = 0
- total_surplus_can_create = 1 + 1 + 0 = 2
- total_deficit = 2
- 2 >= 2, so "Yes" ✓

Example 2: (5,6,1) -> (2,7,2)
- deficit_blue = 0, deficit_violet = 1, deficit_orange = 1
- surplus_blue = 3, surplus_violet = 1, surplus_orange = 0
- surplus_blue//2 = 1, surplus_violet//2 = 0, surplus_orange//2 = 0
- total_surplus_can_create = 1 + 0 + 0 = 1
- total_deficit = 1 + 1 = 2
- 1 >= 2? No → "No" ✓

Example 3: (3,3,3) -> (2,2,2)
- deficit_blue = 0, deficit_violet = 0, deficit_orange = 0
- surplus_blue = 1, surplus_violet = 1, surplus_orange = 1
- surplus_blue//2 = 0, surplus_violet//2 = 0, surplus_orange//2 = 0
- total_surplus_can_create = 0
- total_deficit = 0
- 0 >= 0 → "Yes" ✓

Perfect! This matches all examples.

So the algorithm is:

1. Read a, b, c and x, y, z
2. If a + b + c < x + y + z: print "No"
3. Calculate deficits and surpluses
4. Calculate how many additional spheres we can create from surplus (divided by 2)
5. If total_create_from_surplus >= total_deficit, print "Yes", else "No"

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
x, y, z = map(int, input().split())

# Total spheres constraint
if a + b + c < x + y + z:
    print("No")
else:
    # Calculate deficits (how many more we need in each color)
    deficit_blue = max(0, x - a)
    deficit_violet = max(0, y - b)
    deficit_orange = max(0, z - c)
    
    # Calculate surpluses (how many extra we have in each color)
    surplus_blue = max(0, a - x)
    surplus_violet = max(0, b - y)
    surplus_orange = max(0, c - z)
    
    # Calculate how many additional spheres we can create from surpluses
    # Each pair of spheres can be transformed into one sphere of another color
    can_create = (surplus_blue // 2) + (surplus_violet // 2) + (surplus_orange // 2)
    
    # Total deficit is the sum of deficits
    total_deficit = deficit_blue + deficit_violet + deficit_orange
    
    if can_create >= total_deficit:
        print("Yes")
    else:
        print("No")
```

- a + b + c >= x + y + z
- and the total excess (positive) is at least the total deficit (positive) in a specific way.

After more thought, I found that in each transformation, we can only create one sphere from two spheres of another color. So to cover a deficit of D, we need to create D spheres, which requires 2D spheres from the excess.

Therefore, the total number of spheres available in excess must be at least 2 * total_deficit.

Let me verify:

Example 1:
- total_deficit = (2-4) negative, (1-4) negative, (2-0)=2 → only orange deficit = 2, so total_deficit = 2
- total_excess = (4-2)=2, (4-1)=3, (0-2) negative → total_excess = 2+3=5
- 2 * 2 = 4 <= 5 → "Yes" ✓

Example 2:
- need: blue=2 (have 5), violet=7 (have 6, deficit 1), orange=2 (have 1, deficit 1) → total_deficit = 2
- excess: blue=3, violet=0, orange=0 → total_excess = 3
- 2 * 2 = 4 > 3 → "No" ✓

Example 3:
- need: blue=2 (have 3), violet=2 (have 3), orange=2 (have 3) → all have surplus 1 each, so deficits are 0
- total_deficit = 0
- total_excess = (3-2) *3 = 3
- 2 * 0 = 0 <= 3 → "Yes" ✓

This matches all examples!

Therefore, the solution is:

1. If a + b + c < x + y + z: "No"
2. Otherwise, compute total_deficit = max(0, x-a) + max(0, y-b) + max(0, z-c)
3. Compute total_excess = max(0, a-x) + max(0, b-y) + max(0, c-z)
4. If 2 * total_deficit <= total_excess: "Yes", else "No"

Let me double-check example 3: total_deficit=0, total_excess=3, 0<=3, so "Yes" ✓

Example 2: total_deficit=2, total_excess=3, 4>3, "No" ✓

Example 1: total_deficit=2, total_excess=5, 4<=5, "Yes" ✓

The only edge case might be if total_deficit is 0, then 0<=anything, so always "Yes" if total_count condition is met.

This makes sense because if there's no deficit, we can do nothing or even reduce by transforming, but we only need to meet the minimum, so if we already have at least what we need, we are fine.

Therefore, the solution is:

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
x, y, z = map(int, input().split())

total_available = a + b + c
total_needed = x + y + z

if total_available < total_needed:
    print("No")
else:
    deficit = max(0, x - a) + max(0, y - b) + max(0, z - c)
    excess = max(0, a - x) + max(0, b - y) + max(0, c - z)
    
    if 2 * deficit <= excess:
        print("Yes")
    else:
        print("No")
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We start with `a` blue, `b` violet, and `c` orange spheres
2. We need to reach at least `x` blue, `y` violet, and `z` orange spheres
3. In one move, we can transform **two spheres of the same color** into **one sphere of any other color**

## Key Insights

1. **Transformation rules**: 
   - 2 spheres of color A → 1 sphere of color B
   - This means we lose 1 sphere of color A and gain 1 sphere of color B
   - Net change: -1 for A, +1 for B

2. **Conservation of total spheres**:
   - Each transformation: we start with 2 spheres and end with 1 sphere
   - So we decrease the total count by 1
   - This means the total number of spheres decreases by 1 in each operation

3. **What does this mean for our goal?**
   - If we start with total `a + b + c` spheres and need `x + y + z` spheres
   - Since each operation reduces total by 1, we need: `a + b + c >= x + y + z`
   - This is a necessary condition

4. **Can we actually transform to achieve the required counts?**
   - We can only create spheres by converting two of the same color
   - This means we can only create new spheres when we have enough of one color
   - The key insight is that we can always convert excess spheres of one color into others

5. **When is it impossible?**
   - If we don't have enough total spheres (total starts < total needed)
   - If we need more of a particular color than we have, and we can't create it through transformations

6. **Creating spheres of a specific color**:
   - To get more of a color, we need to convert two spheres of another color
   - But we can only do this if we have at least 2 spheres of that other color
   - The key is that we can always convert excess from any color to any other color

The second line of the input contains three integers, x, y and z (0 ≤ x, y, z ≤ 1 000 000) — the number of blue, violet and orange spheres that he needs to get.


-----Output-----

If the wizard is able to obtain the required numbers of spheres, print "Yes". Otherwise, print "No".


-----Examples-----
Input
4 4 0
2 1 2

Output
Yes

Input
5 6 1
2 7 2

Output
No

Input
3 3 3
2 2 2

Output
Yes



-----Note-----

In the first sample the wizard has 4 blue and 4 violet spheres. In his first action he can turn two blue spheres into one violet one. After that he will have 2 blue and 5 violet spheres. Then he turns 4 violet spheres into 2 orange spheres and he ends up with 2 blue, 1 violet and 2 orange spheres, which is exactly what he needs.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

Carl has `a` blue, `b` violet, and `c` orange spheres. He can transform **two spheres of the same color** into **one sphere of any other color** in one move.

He needs at least `x` blue, `y` violet, and `z` orange spheres.

We need to determine if he can achieve the required amounts.

## Key Insights

1. **Total spheres conservation**: Each transformation reduces the total count by 1 (2 spheres become 1). So the total number of spheres decreases by the number of transformations.

2. **Initial vs Final state**: If we start with `a + b + c` spheres and end with `x + y + z` spheres, then we need exactly `(a + b + c) - (x + y + z)` transformations.

3. **Feasibility conditions**:
   - We can only transform two spheres of the same color into one of another color.
   - This means we can never create more spheres than what we started with, and we can only create spheres by reducing other colors.
   - We need to ensure we have enough "excess" spheres in some colors to perform transformations that help us reach the target.

4. **When is it impossible?** 
   - If the total needed spheres exceeds the total available spheres, it's impossible.
   - If we need more of a color than we have, we need to generate it through transformations.

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
x, y, z = map(int, input().split())

if x + y + z <= a + b + c:
    print("Yes")
else:
    print("No")
```

But I will implement a solution that passes the given examples.

Example 1: (4,4,0) -> (2,1,2): "Yes"
Example 2: (5,6,1) -> (2,7,2): "No"
Example 3: (3,3,3) -> (2,2,2): "Yes"

From example 2, we need more violet and orange, and we have only 3 extra blue, which can only create 1 sphere (using 2 blue), not 2.

So a condition might be: the total number of excess spheres (positive differences) must be at least 2 times the total number of deficit spheres (positive differences).

In example 1:
- excess: (4-2) + (4-1) + (0-2) = 2+3+ (-2) = 3 (only 2+3=5 if we take positive)
- excess = (a-x) + (b-y) + (c-z) but only positive parts: (4-2) + (4-1) + 0 = 2+3 = 5 (since c-z = -2, not positive)
- deficit = (0-2) is negative, so only (2-0) for orange = 2
- excess = (a-x) + (b-y) = 2+3 = 5
- 5 >= 2*2 = 4, true.

In example 2:
- excess = (5-2) + (6-7) + (1-2) = 3 + (-1) + (-1) = 1
- positive excess = 3
- deficit = (2-5) + (7-6) + (2-1) = 0 + 1 + 1 = 2
- 3 >= 2*2 = 4? 3 < 4, false.

In example 3:
- excess = (3-2) for each = 1, so 3
- deficit = (2-3) for each = 0, so 0
- 3 >= 0, true.

So the condition is: the sum of positive (initial_i - final_i) for all i must be >= 2 * the sum of positive (final_i - initial_i) for all i

This makes sense: each sphere we need to create requires 2 spheres of other colors (as we are converting two spheres into one).

In example 1: we need to create 2 orange spheres, so we need 4 spheres of other colors. We have 5 excess (2 from blue, 3 from violet) = 5 >= 4, yes.

In example 2: we need to create 1 violet and 1 orange = 2 spheres, so need 4 spheres of other colors. We have only 3 excess (3 from blue), 3 < 4, no.

In example 3: we need to create 0 spheres, so need 0, have 3 excess >=0, yes.

So the solution is:

1. Compute excess = sum of max(0, a-x) + max(0, b-y) + max(0, c-z)
2. Compute deficit = sum of max(0, x-a) + max(0, y-b) + max(0, z-c)
3. If excess >= 2 * deficit and (x+y+z) <= (a+b+c), then "Yes", else "No"

But is the total constraint needed? In this case, if we have excess >= 2*deficit, then the total change is excess - deficit = (a-x) + (b-y) + (c-z) - (x-a) - (y-b) - (z-c) = 2(a-b-c) + (a+b+c) - (x+y+z) = (a+b+c) - (x+y+z)

We need to have at least the net change, but since we are only creating spheres from pairs, and the net change in total is (a+b+c) - (x+y+z), and we are creating deficit spheres and consuming excess spheres, the total net change is (excess - deficit) = (a+b+c) - (x+y+z)

So if excess >= 2*deficit, then the net change is at least (2*deficit - deficit) = deficit, and we have the excess to cover the net change.

But the net change is (a+b+c) - (x+y+z), which is exactly the number of transformations, and must be non-negative for the total to be sufficient.

In our condition, if excess >= 2*deficit, then since excess - deficit = (a+b+c) - (x+y+z), and excess - deficit >= deficit, so (a+b+c) - (x+y+z) >= deficit, which means (x+y+z) <= a+b+c.

So the condition excess >= 2*deficit implies the total is sufficient.

Therefore, the only condition is: excess >= 2 * deficit

In example 1: excess = (4-2) + (4-1) + 0 = 2+3 = 5, deficit = (2-0) = 2, 5 >= 4, yes.
In example 2: excess = (5-2) + 0 + 0 = 3, deficit = (7-6) + (2-1) = 1+1 = 2, 3 >= 4? no.
In example 3: excess = (3-2)*3 = 3, deficit = 0, 3 >= 0, yes.

So this matches all examples.

Therefore, the solution is:

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
x, y, z = map(int, input().split())

excess = max(0, a - x) + max(0, b - y) + max(0, c - z)
deficit = max(0, x - a) + max(0, y - b) + max(0, z - c)

if excess >= 2 * deficit:
    print("Yes")
else:
    print("No")
```

This should be the correct solution. The excess is the amount of spheres we have in surplus, and each sphere we need to create requires 2 spheres from the surplus, so we need at least 2*deficit excess spheres. The total sphere constraint is automatically satisfied by the net change being non-negative (which is implied by excess >= 2*deficit and the net change being excess - deficit).

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: a=4,b=4,c=0, x=2,y=1,z=2
excess = max(0,4-2) + max(0,4-1) + max(0,0-2) = 2+3+0 = 5
deficit = max(0,2-4) + max(0,1-4) + max(0,2-0) = 0+0+2 = 2
5 >= 4, yes.

Example 2: a=5,b=6,c=1, x=2,y=7,z=2
excess = max(0,5-2) + max(0,6-7) + max(0,1-2) = 3+0+0 = 3
deficit = max(0,2-5) + max(0,7-6) + max(0,2-1) = 0+1+1 = 2
3 >= 4? no, so "No".

Example 3: a=3,b=3,c=3, x=2,y=2,z=2
excess = max(0,3-2)*3 = 3
deficit = 0
3 >= 0, yes.

Perfect! This matches the examples.

So the final solution is:  
Check if the sum of positive differences (surplus) in any color is at least twice the sum of positive differences (deficit) in any color.  
This ensures we have enough spheres to create the required ones.  
Each required sphere requires two spheres from another color.  
The total sphere count constraint is automatically satisfied.  
In the transformation, each move reduces total by 1, but the net change is covered by the surplus and deficit.

Therefore, the code is:

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
x, y, z = map(int, input().split())

excess = max(0, a - x) + max(0, b - y) + max(0, c - z)
deficit = max(0, x - a) + max(0, y - b) + max(0, z - c)

if excess >= 2 * deficit:
    print("Yes")
else:
    print("No")
```

When total_needed ≤ total_initial, total_excess ≥ 0, total_deficit ≥ 0, and total_excess + total_deficit = total_initial - total_needed.

The constraint is that to cover a deficit of d in a color, we need 2d units of excess from other colors.

The total number of units needed to create spheres for deficits is 2 × total_deficit.

We need total_excess ≥ 2 × total_deficit.

Because each conversion to cover a deficit of 1 unit requires 2 units of excess.

Therefore, the final conditions are:
1. x + y + z ≤ a + b + c
2. (a + b + c - (x + y + z)) ≥ 2 * ((x - a) + (y - b) + (z - c)) if (x - a) + (y - b) + (z - c) > 0

Wait, no. total_deficit = (x - a) + (y - b) + (z - c) is not correct because if a > x, then (x-a) is negative.

We need to take absolute values? No.

The total deficit is the sum of positive differences: (max(0, x-a) + max(0, y-b) + max(0, z-c))

And total excess is (a+b+c) - (x+y+z)

For each unit of deficit in a color, we need to create it by converting 2 units from some other color.

So we need total_excess >= 2 * total_deficit, where total_deficit = max(0, x-a) + max(0, y-b) + max(0, z-c)

Ah! This is the key insight!

The parity of the count of each color matters.

When we convert 2 spheres of one color to 1 of another, the parity of that color changes from even to odd or odd to even, but since we are removing 2, the parity of that color remains the same (even-2=even, odd-2=odd).

When we convert 2 spheres of color A to 1 sphere of color B:
- Color A: decreases by 2, so parity doesn't change
- Color B: increases by 1, so parity flips

Therefore, the parity of each color can only change when we create a sphere of that color.

In the final state, the parity of a color is the same as the initial parity of that color, XORed with the number of times we created a sphere of that color (mod 2).

Since each creation of a sphere of a color flips its parity, the final parity of a color is:
- initial_parity XOR (number of times we created a sphere of that color) mod 2

For the final state to be achievable, the number of times we created a sphere of color i must be such that:
final_parity = initial_parity XOR (number of creations of i) mod 2

Therefore, the final_parity must match the initial_parity if and only if the number of creations of i is even.

So we only need to consider the possibility of even or odd number of creations.

But we don't know how many times we create each sphere.

However, the final number of spheres of a color is:
initial_count + (number of times we created it) - (number of times we converted it)

Let c_i be the number of times we create a sphere of color i.
Let d_i be the number of times we convert a sphere of color i.

Then final_count = initial_count + c_i - d_i

And for color i, we need final_count >= need_i.

But we are only interested in the parity.

In the transformation, when we convert from a color, we remove 2, so d_i is the number of times we convert from color i.

When we create a sphere of color i, we add 1.

So final_count_i = initial_i + c_i - d_i

The parity of final_count_i = (initial_i + c_i - d_i) mod 2 = (initial_i + c_i + d_i) mod 2 (since -d_i ≡ d_i mod 2)

This must equal the required parity of final_count_i.

So (initial_i + c_i + d_i) mod 2 = required_i mod 2

But c_i and d_i are non-negative integers.

This means that (c_i + d_i) mod 2 = (required_i - initial_i) mod 2

So the number of creations and conversions of color i must have the same parity as (required_i - initial_i) mod 2.

This seems complex.

After more thought, I found that in this specific problem, the only constraint is that the total number of spheres must not exceed the initial total, and for each color, if the required amount is less than initial, then it's fine; if greater, then we need to create it, and the creation process must maintain the parity constraint.

However, in example 1: 
Initial: blue=4 (even), violet=4 (even), orange=0 (even)
Need: blue=2 (even), violet=1 (odd), orange=2 (even)

For blue: initial even, need even, so (c_blue + d_blue) mod 2 = (2-4) mod 2 = 0 mod 2 = 0
For violet: initial even, need odd, so (c_violet + d_violet) mod 2 = (1-4) mod 2 = 1 mod 2 = 1
For orange: initial even, need even, so (c_orange + d_orange) mod 2 = (2-0) mod 2 = 0 mod 2 = 0

This is possible.

In example 2:
Initial: blue=5 (odd), violet=6 (even), orange=1 (odd)
Need: blue=2 (even), violet=7 (odd), orange=2 (even)

For blue: (2-5) mod 2 = ( -3) mod 2 = 1, so (c_blue + d_blue) mod 2 = 1
For violet: (7-6) mod 2 = 1, so (c_violet + d_violet) mod 2 = 1
For orange: (2-1) mod 2 = 1, so (c_orange + d_orange) mod 2 = 1

So we need the number of creations and conversions for each color to have odd sum.

Is this possible? It should be, but the example says "No".

So the solution is more subtle.

We need to check if it's possible to achieve the required counts through a series of transformations.

Each transformation reduces the total count by 1.

So if (x+y+z) > (a+b+c), then "No".

If (x+y+z) < (a+b+c), then we can reduce the count by (a+b+c) - (x+y+z) transformations.

Each transformation either increases one color by 1 and decreases another by 2 (net -1).

Let the number of transformations be T = (a+b+c) - (x+y+z)

Let the net change for each color be:
- blue: final - initial = (x - a)
- violet: (y - b)
- orange: (z - c)

Let the number of times we create a sphere of blue be c_b, of violet be c_v, of orange be c_o.

Then the net change is:
- blue: -d_b + c_b = x - a
- violet: -d_v + c_v = y - b
- orange: -d_o + c_o = z - c

And the total number of transformations is T = d_b + d_v + d_o = c_b + c_v + c_o

So we have:
 c_b - d_b = x - a
 c_v - d_v = y - b
 c_o - d_o = z - c
 c_b + c_v + c_o = d_b + d_v + d_o = T

From the first three equations:
 c_b = (x - a) + d_b
 c_v = (y - b) + d_v
 c_o = (z - c) + d_o

Substitute into the fourth:
 (x-a) + d_b + (y-b) + d_v + (z-c) + d_o = d_b + d_v + d_o
 (x+y+z) - (a+b+c) + (d_b + d_v + d_o) = d_b + d_v + d_o
 (x+y+z) - (a+b+c) = 0

But this is only true if (x+y+z) = (a+b+c), which is not the case.

 specifically, let min_blue = (a-x +1)//2 if a>x, else 0  (since 2*f_blue >= a-x, so f_blue >= (a-x+1)//2 when a-x is odd, and (a-x)//2 when a-x is even)

 actually, if a-x >0, then f_blue >= ceil((a-x)/2) = (a-x+1)//2
 if a-x <=0, then f_blue >=0

 similarly for others.

 then we need min_blue + min_violet + min_orange <= T

 then it is possible.

 in example 1: a=4,b=4,c=0; x=2,y=1,z=2
 a-x = 2, ceil(2/2)=1
 b-y = 3, ceil(3/2)=2
 c-z = -2, so min_orange =0
 min_sum = 1+2+0 = 3
 T = 3
 3 <= 3, so "Yes"

 in example 2: a=5,b=6,c=1; x=2,y=7,z=2
 a-x = 3, ceil(3/2)=2
 b-y = -1, so min_violet =0
 c-z = -1, so min_orange =0
 min_sum = 2+0+0 = 2
 T = 1
 2 <= 1? no, so "No"

 in example 3: a=3,b=3,c=3; x=2,y=2,z=2
 a-x=1, ceil(1/2)=1
 b-y=1, ceil(1/2)=1
 c-z=1, ceil(1/2)=1
 min_sum = 3
 T = (3+3+3) - (2+2+2) = 9-6=3
 3<=3, so "Yes"

 and the examples match.

 therefore, the algorithm is:

 read a,b,c
 read x,y,z

 if (x+y+z) > (a+b+c):
     print "No"
 else:
     T = (a+b+c) - (x+y+z)
     # min_f for blue: if a > x, then ceil((a-x)/2), else 0
     min_blue = (a - x + 1) // 2 if a > x else 0
     min_violet = (b - y + 1) // 2 if b > y else 0
     min_orange = (c - z + 1) // 2 if c > z else 0
     if min_blue + min_violet + min_orange <= T:
         print "Yes"
     else:
         print "No"

 but in the case a==x, (a-x+1)//2 = (0+1)//2 = 0 when a==x, so it's (0+1)//2 = 0 in integer division in python? let's check: (0+1)//2 = 1//2 = 0 in Python.

 so for a==x, (a-x+1)//2 = (0+1)//2 = 0 in integer division.

 for a>x: if a-x=1, (1+1)//2 = 2//2=1, which is ceil(1/2)=1
 if a-x=2, (2+1)//2 = 3//2=1, but ceil(2/2)=1, so 1, correct
 if a-x=3, (3+1)//2 = 4//2=2, ceil(3/2)=2, correct.

 so ceil(n/2) = (n+1)//2 for n>=0.

 therefore, we can write: min_blue = (a - x + 1) // 2 if a > x else 0

 but when a>x, (a-x) >=1, and (a-x+1)//2 is ceil((a-x)/2)

 when a<=x, min_blue=0.

 so the code is:

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
x, y, z = map(int, input().split())

if x + y + z > a + b + c:
    print("No")
else:
    T = (a + b + c) - (x + y + z)
    # minimum number of times we need to convert from each color
    min_blue = (a - x + 1) // 2 if a > x else 0
    min_violet = (b - y + 1) // 2 if b > y else 0
    min_orange = (c - z + 1) // 2 if c > z else 0
    if min_blue + min_violet + min_orange <= T:
        print("Yes")
    else:
        print("No")
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We start with `a` blue, `b` violet, and `c` orange spheres
2. We need to reach at least `x` blue, `y` violet, and `z` orange spheres
3. In one move, we can take two spheres of the same color and transform them into one sphere of any other color
4. We need to determine if it's possible to achieve the required amounts

## Key Insights

1. **Conservation of total spheres**: Each transformation removes exactly one sphere (2 spheres → 1 sphere). So the total number of spheres decreases by 1 each time.

2. **Initial total vs Final total**: 
   - Initial total: `a + b + c`
   - Final total: `x + y + z`
   - We need: `a + b + c >= x + y + z` (since we can only reduce the total)

3. **Individual constraints**: We need to ensure we can create enough spheres of each color when needed.

4. **The crucial insight**: The transformation operation means we can convert from any color to any other color. The key constraint is that we can only reduce the total count, so we need enough total spheres initially.

5. **Negative excess handling**: If we have excess spheres of a particular color, we can use them to create other colors. However, we can't create spheres "from nothing" - we need to have enough total spheres.

6. **Sufficient condition**: The main constraint is that the total spheres must be sufficient: `a + b + c >= x + y + z`.

7. **Additional constraint**: We also need to ensure that we don't have "negative" requirements. If we need more of a color than we currently have, we can only create it by converting other colors.

Total excess = (4-2) + (4-1) + (0-2) = 2 + 3 + 0 = 5 (wait, the last is negative)
Excess = blue: 4-2=2, violet: 4-1=3, orange: 0-2 = negative → 0
Total excess = 5

T = initial - final = 8 - 5 = 3
D = 2
2D = 4
T = 3
3 >= 4? No.

But we know solution exists.

Let me trace the solution:
Start: (4,4,0)
Goal: (2,1,2)

Operation 1: 2 blue → 1 violet → (2,5,0)
Operation 2: 2 blue → 1 white? No, only 3 colors.
Operation 2: 4 violet → 2 orange → (2,3,2) → not matching
Operation 2: 2 violet → 1 orange → (2,3,1)
Operation 3: 2 violet → 1 orange → (2,1,2)

Total operations: 3
Final: (2,1,2) ✓

Spheres consumed: 
- Operation 1: 2 blue → 1 violet: consume 2 blue, produce 1 violet
- Operation 2: 2 violet → 1 orange: consume 2 violet, produce 1 orange
- Operation 3: 2 violet → 1 orange: consume 2 violet, produce 1 orange

Total consumption: 
- blue: 2 spheres consumed
- violet: 4 spheres consumed
- orange: 0 consumed

Total spheres consumed: 6

But initial spheres: 8, final: 5, so 3 spheres consumed. Contradiction.

Each operation consumes 1 sphere (2 in, 1 out). So 3 operations consume 3 spheres.

The consumption is per operation, not per sphere.

When we convert 2 of one color to 1 of another, we consume 1 sphere (net).

The key is that we are not consuming 2 spheres of A to make 1 of B - we are using 2 of A and producing 1 of B, so net consumption is 1 sphere.

The spheres consumed in terms of color are:
- We lose 2 of color A, gain 1 of color B.

So for the deficit in orange: we need to create 2 orange spheres.
Each creation of an orange sphere requires consuming 2 spheres of some other color.

So to create 2 orange spheres, we need to consume 4 spheres from other colors.

The other colors are blue and violet.

We have blue: 4, need 2 → can give up 2 (surplus of 2)
We have violet: 4, need 1 → can give up 3 (surplus of 3)
Total available to give up: 5

We need to give up 4 spheres (to create 2 orange spheres).

Yes! 5 >= 4, so possible.

So the constraint is: the total spheres we need to "give up" (i.e., convert from other colors) is 2 * D, where D is the total deficit.

The total spheres available to give up is the total surplus, which is T.

Therefore, we need: T >= 2D.

In example 1:
- D = 2 (deficit in orange)
- T = 3
- 3 >= 2*2 = 4? 3 >= 4? No → should be "No", but expected is "Yes"

Still not matching.

Wait, the deficit is not only in orange. We have violet: 4, need 1 → excess of 3, blue: 4, need 2 → excess of 2.

But we are creating 2 orange spheres, each requiring 2 spheres of another color, so total 4 spheres.

We have total excess of 5, so 5 >= 4 → yes.

So the available spheres to give up is the sum of surpluses, not T.

T = (a+b+c) - (x+y+z) = 3
But sum of surpluses = (4-2) + (4-1) + (0-2) = 2 + 3 + 0 = 5

So we need sum of surpluses >= 2 * sum of deficits.

In example 1: sum(sup) = 5, 2 * sum(def) = 4, 5 >= 4 ✓

In example 2: 
- sum(sup) = (5-2) + (6-7) + (1-2) = 3 + 0 + 0 = 3
- sum(def) = (2-5)→0 + (7-6)→1 + (2-1)→1 = 2
- 2 * sum(def) = 4, 3 >= 4? No → "No" ✓

In example 3:
- sum(sup) = (3-2) + (3-2) + (3-2) = 1+1+1 = 3
- sum(def) = 0
- 2*0 = 0, 3 >= 0 → "Yes" ✓

Perfect!

So the algorithm is:
1. Calculate total_surplus = max(0, a-x) + max(0, b-y) + max(0, c-z)
2. Calculate total_deficit = max(0, x-a) + max(0, y-b) + max(0, z-c)
3. If total_surplus >= 2 * total_deficit: print "Yes"
4. Else: print "No"

Note that total_surplus + total_deficit = (a+b+c) - (x+y+z) = T >= 0, so we don't need to check that separately.

Therefore, the solution is:

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
x, y, z = map(int, input().split())

total_surplus = max(0, a - x) + max(0, b - y) + max(0, c - z)
total_deficit = max(0, x - a) + max(0, y - b) + max(0, z - c)

if total_surplus >= 2 * total_deficit:
    print("Yes")
else:
    print("No")
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a, b, c spheres of blue, violet, and orange colors respectively
2. We need to achieve at least x, y, z spheres of blue, violet, and orange respectively
3. In one move, we can transform two spheres of the same color into one sphere of any other color
4. We need to determine if it's possible to reach the required configuration

## Key Insights

1. **Total sphere conservation**: Each transformation converts 2 spheres into 1 sphere, so we lose 1 sphere per operation. Therefore, the total number of spheres decreases by 1 each time.

2. **Initial vs Final total**: If we start with `a + b + c` spheres and need `x + y + z` spheres, we need to check if `a + b + c >= x + y + z`. If not, it's impossible.

3. **Direction of operations**: 
   - To increase a color, we need to convert spheres from other colors into that color
   - To decrease a color, we can convert two of that color into one of another color

4. **Critical insight**: We can only increase a count if we have excess spheres in other colors. For example, to increase blue spheres, we need to convert violet or orange spheres into blue.

5. **Sufficiency conditions**: 
   - We need enough total spheres: `a + b + c >= x + y + z`
   - For each color, we need to ensure we can make up the deficit using excess in other colors

The key point is that for each color, the final count is initial count plus the net change from operations (produced minus consumed).

The number of operations that produce a color (p_i) and consume a color (c_i) with c_i being the number of operations that used that color to consume (each such operation consumes 2 spheres).

For color i:
- final_i = initial_i - 2 * (number of operations that consumed i) + (number of operations that produced i)

And the sum over i of (number of operations that consumed i) = total operations D

Let D = (a+b+c) - (x+y+z)

For each color i, let c_i be the number of operations that consumed color i (so each such operation consumes 2 spheres of color i)
Let p_i be the number of operations that produced color i (each such operation produces 1 sphere of color i)

Then: for each i: x_i = a_i - 2c_i + p_i

Also: c_1 + c_2 + c_3 = D

And p_1 + p_2 + p_3 = D (because each operation produces exactly one sphere)

From the equation: p_i = x_i - a_i + 2c_i

Since p_i ≥ 0, we need: x_i - a_i + 2c_i ≥ 0

So: 2c_i ≥ a_i - x_i

So c_i ≥ max(0, (a_i - x_i) / 2)

Similarly, c_i ≥ 0, so c_i ≥ max(0, (a_i - x_i) / 2)

We also have: c_1 + c_2 + c_3 = D

So we need: max(0, (a-b)/2) + max(0, (b-y)/2) + max(0, (c-z)/2) ≤ D

But only if the values are integers, and since c_i must be integers, and (a_i - x_i) may be odd, we need to be careful.

Actually, since we are taking floor or ceil, but the condition is that c_i >= ceil((a_i - x_i)/2) if a_i > x_i.

For example, if a_i - x_i = 3, then c_i >= 2 (since 2*1 = 2 < 3, 2*2 = 4 >= 3)

So c_i >= ceil((a_i - x_i) / 2)

And if a_i <= x_i, then a_i - x_i <= 0, so c_i >= 0.

So the condition is: sum over i of max(0, ceil((a_i - x_i) / 2)) <= D

But in the example: a=4,b=4,c=0, x=2,y=1,z=2, D=3
- Blue: a-x = 2, ceil(2/2) = 1
- Violet: b-y = 3, ceil(3/2) = 2
- Orange: c-z = -2, max(0, ceil(-2/2)) = 0
- Sum = 1+2+0 = 3 ≤ 3 ✓

Example 2: a=5,b=6,c=1, x=2,y=7,z=2, D=12-11=1
- Blue: 5-2=3, ceil(3/2)=2
- Violet: 6-7=-1, ceil(-1/2)=0
- Orange: 1-2=-1, ceil(-1/2)=0
- Sum = 2+0+0 = 2 > 1 → No

- Violet: 3-2=1, ceil(1/2)=1

- Sum = 1+1+1 = 3 ≤ 3 ✓

1. Read a,b,c and x,y,z

3. If D < 0: return "No" (impossible to reduce total)

Now, in code, for a non-negative number n, ceil(n/2) = (n + 1) // 2 if n > 0, but if n==0, ceil(0/2)=0.

For n >= 0, ceil(n/2) = (n + 1) // 2 when n > 0, and 0 when n==0.

Actually: 
- n=0: ceil(0/2)=0
- n=1: ceil(1/2)=1
- n=2: ceil(2/2)=1
- n=3: ceil(3/2)=2

So for n>=0: ceil(n/2) = (n + 1) // 2

n=0: (0+1)//2 = 1//2 = 0
n=1: (1+1)//2 = 2//2 = 1
n=2: (2+1)//2 = 3//2 = 1
n=3: (3+1)//2 = 4//2 = 2
Yes! This works.

So for each color, if a_i > x_i, then ceil((a_i - x_i) / 2) = (a_i - x_i + 1) // 2
Otherwise 0.

But we can write: max(0, (a_i - x_i + 1) // 2) for i in (0,1,2)

Actually, when a_i <= x_i, (a_i - x_i) <= 0, so (a_i - x_i + 1) // 2 might be negative or zero.

For example, a_i - x_i = -1: (-1+1)//2 = 0//2 = 0
a_i - x_i = -2: (-2+1)//2 = -1//2 = -1 in Python? No, in Python floor division for negative is floor.

In Python, (-1)//2 = -1, (-2)//2 = -1.

So (a_i - x_i + 1) // 2 when a_i - x_i < 0 might be negative.

We need to take max(0, (a_i - x_i + 1) // 2)

For a_i - x_i = -1: (-1+1)//2 = 0//2 = 0
For a_i - x_i = -2: (-2+1)//2 = -1//2 = -1 → take max(0, -1) = 0
For a_i - x_i = -3: (-3+1)//2 = -2//2 = -1 → 0

So it works.

Therefore, the code is:

1. Read a,b,c and x,y,z
2. D = a+b+c - (x+y+z)
3. If D < 0: print "No" and exit
4. total_needed = 0
5. for each of the three colors:
   - diff = a_i - x_i
   - needed = max(0, (diff + 1) // 2)
   - total_needed += needed
6. if total_needed <= D: print "Yes" else print "No"

Let me test with the examples:

Example 1: a=4,b=4,c=0, x=2,y=1,z=2
D = 8-5 = 3
- Blue: diff = 4-2 = 2, (2+1)//2 = 3//2 = 1, needed=1
- Violet: diff = 4-1 = 3, (3+1)//2 = 4//2 = 2, needed=2
- Orange: diff = 0-2 = -2, (-2+1)//2 = -1//2 = -1, max(0,-1)=0
- total_needed = 1+2+0 = 3 <= 3 → Yes

Example 2: a=5,b=6,c=1, x=2,y=7,z=2
D = 12-11 = 1
- Blue: 5-2=3, (3+1)//2 = 4//2 = 2
- Violet: 6-7=-1, (-1+1)//2 = 0//2 = 0
- Orange: 1-2=-1, (-1+1)//2 = 0//2 = 0
- total_needed = 2 > 1 → No

Example 3: a=3,b=3,c=3, x=2,y=2,z=2
D = 9-6 = 3
- Each: diff=1, (1+1)//2 = 2//2 = 1
- total_needed = 3 <= 3 → Yes

All match.

Edge cases: if D=0, then total_needed must be 0, which means no excess in any color.

If a_i <= x_i for all i, then total_needed=0, so we need D>=0, which is true if a+b+c >= x+y+z.

If any a_i > x_i, we need to consume at least ceil((a_i-x_i)/2) operations from that color.

So the solution is:

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
x, y, z = map(int, input().split())

total_spheres = a + b + c
needed_spheres = x + y + z

if total_spheres < needed_spheres:
    print("No")
else:
    D = total_spheres - needed_spheres  # number of operations needed
    
    # Calculate minimum operations needed to consume excess spheres
    # For each color, if we have more than needed, we need to consume them
    # Each consumption of 2 spheres from a color requires one operation that consumes that color
    # So for excess diff, we need ceil(diff/2) operations from that color
    operations_needed = 0
    
    # Blue
    diff_blue = a - x
    if diff_blue > 0:
        operations_needed += (diff_blue + 1) // 2
    
    # Violet
    diff_violet = b - y
    if diff_violet > 0:
        operations_needed += (diff_violet + 1) // 2
    
    # Orange
    diff_orange = c - z
    if diff_orange > 0:
        operations_needed += (diff_orange + 1) // 2
    
    if operations_needed <= D:
        print("Yes")
    else:
        print("No")
```

2. `e_a//2 + e_b//2 + e_c//2 >= T` where `T = (a+b+c) - (x+y+z)`

But actually, this might not be sufficient. We also need to ensure that we can distribute the created spheres to meet the target deficits.

Wait, let me go back to the examples.

Example 1: 4,4,0 → 2,1,2
- a=4,b=4,c=0 → x=2,y=1,z=2
- Total: 8 → 5, T=3
- Excess: blue: 4-2=2, violet: 4-1=3, orange: 0-2=-2 (negative, no excess)
- Available pairs: 2//2 + 3//2 = 1 + 1 = 2
- Need T=3 pairs, but only have 2 available → should be impossible?

But expected output is "Yes".

I made a mistake in the deficit/credo analysis.

Actually, the conversion works in both directions. We can convert any two spheres of the same color into one of any other color. This means we can move excess from one color to another.

The total number of transformations is T = (a+b+c) - (x+y+z).

For each transformation, we consume 2 spheres of one color and produce 1 sphere of another color.

The net effect is a reduction of 1 sphere in total.

The key constraint is that we need enough spheres to perform the T transformations. Specifically, for each color, the amount by which we decrease its count must be at least the amount we need to reduce it below its initial, but with the constraint that for any transformation, we need 2 spheres of the same color.

So for color i, if we start with `initial[i]` and end with `target[i]`, then we must have `initial[i] >= target[i]` OR we must create `target[i] - initial[i]` through transformations from other colors.

If `initial[i] < target[i]`, we need to create `target[i] - initial[i]` spheres of color i through transformations from other colors.

Each transformation produces one sphere of a color, so we need exactly `sum(max(0, target[i] - initial[i]))` transformations that produce spheres of our desired colors.

But the total number of transformations is exactly `(a+b+c) - (x+y+z)`.

And this must equal the total deficit, because each transformation produces one sphere which helps to meet a deficit.

Therefore:
1. `a + b + c >= x + y + z` (total spheres constraint)
2. `sum(max(0, x - a, y - b, z - c)) == (a + b + c) - (x + y + z)` 

Let me verify with example 1:
- initial: (4,4,0), target: (2,1,2)
- deficit: blue: 0 (4>=2), violet: 3 (4>=1), orange: 2 (0<2)
- total deficit: 2
- total transformation count: 8-5 = 3
- 2 != 3 → should be "No", but expected is "Yes"

This is wrong.

I see the issue. The total spheres we create through transformations is exactly T = (a+b+c) - (x+y+z), and this must be distributed to meet the deficits. 

But the sum of deficits (amount we need to create) is exactly T, because each transformation creates one sphere that contributes to some deficit.

In example 1:
- We need 2 more orange spheres
- We have excess of blue (2) and violet (3)
- We can convert 2 blue to 1 orange (using 2 blue, creating 1 orange)
- We can convert 2 violet to 1 orange (using 2 violet, creating 1 orange)
- Total created: 2 orange spheres
- Total transformations: 2, but we only have 3 total spheres to reduce (8-5=3)

Wait, there's a contradiction.

Let me trace through the example:
Start: 4 blue, 4 violet, 0 orange → total 8
Action 1: 2 blue → 1 violet → now: 2 blue, 5 violet, 0 orange → total 7
Action 2: 2 violet → 1 orange → 2 blue, 3 violet, 1 orange → total 6
Action 3: 2 violet → 1 orange → 2 blue, 1 violet, 2 orange → total 5

So 3 transformations, total spheres reduced from 8 to 5.

We created 2 orange spheres (from 0 to 2), and we also had to reduce blue from 4 to 2 (excess of 2), and violet from 4 to 1 (excess of 3).

The total spheres created are 2 (orange), which equals the total deficit in orange.

But the total number of transformations is 3, not 2.

The total number of spheres created is 3 (2 from violet to orange and 1 from blue to violet), but we only needed 2 orange spheres.

Actually, in transformation 1, we converted 2 blue to 1 violet, which increased violet count. This helped us meet the violet target which was lower.

The key insight from the problem is that we can transform any two spheres of the same color into one of any other color. We need to end up with at least x blue, y violet, z orange.

The necessary and sufficient conditions are:

1. `a + b + c >= x + y + z` (total spheres constraint)
2. For each color, we need to be able to achieve the target. We can only create a sphere of a color if we have at least 2 spheres of some other color to transform.

But actually, we can always transform from any color with at least 2 spheres to create one of any other color.

The only real constraint is that we can't create more spheres than available through transformations from excess.

Let me define:
- Total transformations needed: T = (a + b + c) - (x + y + z)
- Total spheres created: T (one per transformation)
- We need to distribute these T spheres to meet the deficits in each color.

The total deficit is D = (x - a) if x > a, else 0, plus similarly for other colors.

But D = sum(max(0, x-a, y-b, z-c))

And we need to create exactly D spheres through transformations, so we need T = D.

But in example 1: D = (2-4) negative, (1-4) negative, (2-0)=2, so D=2. T = 8-5=3. 2 != 3 → should be "No", but expected is "Yes".

This is clearly wrong.

I see the error. In the transformation, when we convert two spheres of one color to one sphere of another color, we are not necessarily creating a sphere that helps with a deficit. We are just moving spheres.

The net effect on any particular color can be positive or negative.

The only constraint is that we can only perform transformations if we have at least 2 spheres of the same color.

The necessary and sufficient condition is:
- `a + b + c >= x + y + z`
- For each color, the amount of excess we have must be at least enough to cover the transformations needed to create spheres of other colors, but since we can choose which color to convert to, it's more subtle.

After some research on similar problems, I recall that in such problems, the conditions are:

1. `a + b + c >= x + y + z` 
2. `a >= x - (something)` but the standard solution is simpler.

Actually, thinking again: in each transformation, we reduce the total count by 1, and we can convert any two of the same color to one of any other color.

The key insight from known problems is that if the total number of spheres is sufficient, and for each color, the number of spheres we have is at least the number we need, or if we have excess, we can always convert it to meet deficits as long as we have enough total excess.

But in example 1, we have more violet than needed, more blue than needed, and need more orange.

We can convert pairs from blue or violet to create orange.

We need to create 2 orange spheres, so we need 4 spheres from blue or violet to convert.

We have 4 blue and 4 violet, so we have 4 (blue) + 4 (violet) = 8 spheres. We only need 4 to create 2 orange spheres (since 2 spheres → 1 orange).

So we have enough.

The number of transformations we need to perform is exactly the total reduction in spheres: T = (a+b+c) - (x+y+z) = 3.

For each color, the net change in count is (target - initial). 

For blue: 2-4 = -2
For violet: 1-4 = -3  
For orange: 2-0 = +2

The total net change is -2 -3 +2 = -3, which should equal -T = -3. Correct.

The constraint is that we can only transform if we have at least 2 spheres of a given color at the time.

But as long as at any point we have at least 2 spheres of some color, we can perform a transformation.

In practice, if we have enough total excess, and enough total spheres, it should be possible.

I found a known solution for this type of problem:

The conditions are:
1. `a + b + c >= x + y + z`
2. `a >= x - (b + c - something)` 

After checking known similar problems, the solution is:

We can always achieve the target if:
- `a + b + c >= x + y + z`
- `a >= x - (b + c)` is not right.

Let me think differently. We can create spheres only by transforming pairs from one color to another. So to create a sphere of a color, we need to have at least 2 spheres of another color.

The total number of spheres we can create from excess is limited by the total excess divided by 2.

But in example 1, total excess = (4-2) + (4-1) = 2 + 3 = 5
Total pairs available = 5 // 2 = 2 (since 2+3=5, pairs=2)

We need to create 2 orange spheres, which requires 4 spheres from other colors (2 spheres per sphere created).

We have 5 excess spheres, which can provide 2 pairs (4 spheres), which is not enough for 4 spheres.

But in the solution, they used 2 blue and 2 violet to create 2 orange spheres (using 4 spheres), so the excess is 5, which is more than 4, so it should be possible.

We need 4 spheres to create 2 orange spheres (2 sphere pairs).

We have 5 excess spheres (2 blue and 3 violet), which can be used to form 2 pairs (using 4 spheres), which is sufficient.

So the number of spheres needed to create a sphere of a color is 2.

For each deficit of 1 in a color, we need to use 2 spheres from another color.

So total spheres needed from other colors = 2 * total_deficit.

We have total excess = sum(max(0, initial - target)).

We need: `sum(max(0, initial - target)) >= 2 * sum(max(0, target - initial))`

And also: `a + b + c >= x + y + z`

Let me test with example 1:
- excess = (4-2) + (4-1) = 2 + 3 = 5
- deficit = (2-0) = 2  
- 2 * deficit = 4
- 5 >= 4 → true
- total spheres: 8 >= 5 → true
→ "Yes" ✓

Test example 2: 5,6,1 → 2,7,2
- initial: 5,6,1 → target: 2,7,2
- excess: (5-2)=3, (6-7)=0 (negative), (1-2)=0 (negative) → excess = 3
- deficit: (2-5)=0, (7-6)=1, (2-1)=1 → deficit = 2
- 2 * deficit = 4
- 3 >= 4 → false → "No" ✓

Test example 3: 3,3,3 → 2,2,2
- excess: (3-2)=1 for each → total excess = 3
- deficit: 0
- 2 * deficit = 0
- 3 >= 0 → true
- total spheres: 9 >= 6 → true
→ "Yes" ✓

Perfect! This matches all examples.

## Final Algorithm

1. Read a, b, c and x, y, z
2. Check if a + b + c >= x + y + z
3. Calculate excess = max(0, a - x) + max(0, b - y) + max(0, c - z)
4. Calculate deficit = max(0, x - a) + max(0, y - b) + max(0, z - c)
5. If excess < 2 * deficit, return "No"
6. Otherwise, return "Yes"

```python
# Read input
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
x, y, z = map(int, input().split())

# Check total spheres constraint
if a + b + c < x + y + z:
    print("No")
else:
    # Calculate excess (amount above target in each color)
    excess = max(0, a - x) + max(0, b - y) + max(0, c - z)
    # Calculate deficit (amount below target in each color)
    deficit = max(0, x - a) + max(0, y - b) + max(0, z - c)
    
    # We need at least 2 spheres for every 1 sphere we create
    if excess >= 2 * deficit:
        print("Yes")
    else:
        print("No")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to count how much coffee is needed based on a list of events
2. Different events require different amounts of coffee:
   - Lowercase events: 1 coffee each
   - Uppercase events: 2 coffees each
3. Specific events:
   - 'cw' (come here to solve kata): 1 coffee (lowercase)
   - 'dog' or 'cat': 1 coffee each (lowercase)
   - 'movie': 1 coffee (lowercase)
4. Any other arbitrary strings should be ignored
5. If total coffee exceeds 3, return "You need extra sleep"

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Identify the events and their coffee requirements
- Any event that is lowercase: 1 coffee
- Any event that is uppercase: 2 coffees
- We need to check if the event is one of the special cases (cw, dog, cat, movie) - but the problem states that lowercase events need 1 coffee and uppercase need 2, so we can just check case

### Step 2: Algorithm
1. Initialize a coffee counter to 0
2. For each event in the input list:
   - If the event is uppercase, add 2 to coffee count
   - If the event is lowercase, add 1 to coffee count
   - If the event is neither (or doesn't match any special case), ignore it
3. After processing all events, check if coffee count > 3
4. If yes, return "You need extra sleep"
5. If no, return the coffee count

### Step 3: Implementation details
- We can use `isupper()` and `islower()` to check the case
- We can ignore any event that doesn't match our criteria (but the problem says to ignore arbitrary strings)

### Step 4: Code implementation

```python
def how_much_coffee(events):
    coffee_needed = 0
    
    for event in events:
        if event.isupper():
            coffee_needed += 2
        elif event.islower():
            coffee_needed += 1
        # Ignore arbitrary strings (they don't have isupper() or islower() properly defined)
        # But the problem says to ignore arbitrary strings, so we just skip them
    
    if coffee_needed > 3:
        return "You need extra sleep"
    
    return coffee_needed
```

Actually, looking at the examples, it seems like we just need to check the case. Any string that is uppercase gives 2 coffees, any lowercase gives 1 coffee, and arbitrary strings (not matching any specific event) should be ignored.

But the problem doesn't specify that we need to check for specific events like 'cw', 'dog', 'cat', 'movie'. It just says we need 1 coffee for lowercase and 2 for uppercase.

Let me re-read: "If it is downcase/lowercase you need 1 coffee by events and if it is uppercase you need 2 coffees."

This suggests the rule is simply based on case, not on the specific event content.

So the solution is straightforward:

```python

    coffee_needed = 0
    
    for event in events:
        if event.isupper():
            coffee_needed += 2
        elif event.islower():
            coffee_needed += 1

        # But if it's neither upper nor lower, we just ignore it
        # This handles arbitrary strings that might not be properly categorized
    
    if coffee_needed > 3:
        return "You need extra sleep"
    
    return coffee_needed
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to count how much coffee is needed based on different events
2. Events can be:
   - 'cw' (come here to solve katas) → 1 coffee
   - 'dog' or 'cat' → 1 coffee each
   - 'movie' → 1 coffee
   - Any other string → ignore
3. Case sensitivity:
   - Lowercase events → 1 coffee each
   - Uppercase events → 2 coffees each
4. If total coffee exceeds 3, return "You need extra sleep"
5. Otherwise, return the total number of coffees

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Input**: Array of events (strings)
2. **Process each event**:
   - Check if it's one of the valid events ('cw', 'dog', 'cat', 'movie')
   - If valid, count coffee based on case:
     - If lowercase → 1 coffee
     - If uppercase → 2 coffees
   - Ignore any other events
3. **Sum up** all coffee counts
4. **Check threshold**: if sum > 3, return "You need extra sleep"
5. **Otherwise**, return the sum

## Edge Cases to Consider
- Empty input array → return 0
- All uppercase events → might exceed 3
- Mixed case events
- Events with different cases

## Implementation

```python
def how_much_coffee(events):
    coffee_needed = 0
    
    for event in events:
        # Convert event to lowercase for comparison
        event_lower = event.lower()
        
        # Check for valid events
        if event_lower == 'cw' or event_lower == 'dog' or event_lower == 'cat' or event_lower == 'movie':
            # If original event is lowercase, need 1 coffee
            # If original event is uppercase, need 2 coffees
            if event.islower():
                coffee_needed += 1
            else:
                coffee_needed += 2
                
    # Check if coffee needed exceeds 3
    if coffee_needed > 3:
        return "You need extra sleep"
    else:
        return coffee_needed
```

The solution works by:
1. Iterating through each event in the input list
2. Converting each event to lowercase to check if it's a valid event type
3. For valid events, checking if the original event was uppercase or lowercase to determine coffee count
4. Summing up the coffee needs
5. Returning the appropriate result based on whether the total exceeds 3

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to count how much coffee is needed based on a list of events
2. Different events require different amounts of coffee:
   - Standard events (lowercase): 1 coffee each
   - Uppercase events: 2 coffees each

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the events and their coffee requirements
- 'cw', 'dog', 'cat', 'movie' (in lowercase): 1 coffee each
- Any of these in uppercase: 2 coffees each
- Other strings: ignore

### Step 2: Algorithm
1. Initialize a coffee counter to 0
2. For each event in the events list:
   - Convert the event to lowercase (to handle case sensitivity)
   - Check if it's one of the specific events ('cw', 'dog', 'cat', 'movie')
   - If it matches a specific event, add coffee based on original case:
     - If original event is uppercase, add 2 coffees
     - If original event is lowercase, add 1 coffee
   - Otherwise, ignore it

- Can process each event individually and check both the value and its case

### Step 4: Code Implementation

```python
def how_much_coffee(events):
    coffee_needed = 0
    
    for event in events:
        # Check if the event is one of the relevant types
        event_lower = event.lower()
        
        if event_lower == 'cw' or event_lower == 'dog' or event_lower == 'cat' or event_lower == 'movie':
            # For relevant events, add coffee based on original case
            if event.isupper():
                coffee_needed += 2
            else:
                coffee_needed += 1
        # For any other event, ignore it (do nothing)
    
    # Check if we need extra sleep
    if coffee_needed > 3:
        return "You need extra sleep"
    
    return coffee_needed
```

    for event in events:
        # Check if the event is one of the relevant types
        event_lower = event.lower()
        
        if event_lower == 'cw' or event_lower == 'dog' or event_lower == 'cat' or event_lower == 'movie':
            # For relevant events, add coffee based on original case
            if event.isupper():
                coffee_needed += 2
            else:
                coffee_needed += 1
        # For any other event, ignore it (do nothing)
    
    # Check if we need extra sleep
    if coffee_needed > 3:
        return "You need extra sleep"
    
    return coffee_needed

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to count how much coffee is needed based on different events
2. Events can be:
   - 'cw' (come here to solve kata) → 1 coffee
   - 'dog' or 'cat' → 1 coffee each
   - 'movie' → 1 coffee
   - Any other arbitrary string → ignore
3. Coffee calculation:
   - Lowercase events → 1 coffee per event
   - Uppercase events → 2 coffees per event
4. If total coffee exceeds 3, return "You need extra sleep"

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Parse the input**: We receive an array of events as strings
2. **Process each event**:
   - Check if it's one of the recognized events ('cw', 'dog', 'cat', 'movie')
   - If it is, determine if it's uppercase or lowercase
   - Add the appropriate coffee count (1 for lowercase, 2 for uppercase)
   - Ignore any other arbitrary strings
3. **Sum up the coffee count**
4. **Return result**:
   - If count > 3, return "You need extra sleep"
   - Otherwise, return the count

## Implementation Details

- We need to check the event type first (is it one of our recognized events?)
- Then check if it's uppercase or lowercase
- For events like 'Dog' or 'DOG', we count it as uppercase (2 coffees)
- For 'cw', 'dog', 'cat', 'movie' in lowercase, we count as 1 coffee each

## Code Implementation

```python
def how_much_coffee(events):
    coffee_count = 0
    
    for event in events:
        # Check if event is one of our recognized events
        if event.lower() in ['cw', 'dog', 'cat', 'movie']:
            # If the original event is uppercase, we need 2 coffees
            # If it's lowercase, we need 1 coffee
            if event.isupper():
                coffee_count += 2
            else:
                coffee_count += 1
        # Ignore any other arbitrary strings
        # (since they're not in our recognized list)
    
    # Return result based on coffee count
    if coffee_count > 3:
        return "You need extra sleep"
    else:
        return coffee_count
```

The solution works as follows:
- For each event, we convert it to lowercase to check if it's one of our recognized events
- We use `isupper()` to check if the original event was uppercase (which gives us 2 coffees)
- We only process recognized events; everything else is ignored
- Finally, we check if the total exceeds 3 and return the appropriate message

## Problem Understanding

2. Events types and their coffee requirements:
   - 'cw' (come here to solve kata): 1 coffee (lowercase) 
   - 'dog' or 'cat' (animal waking up): 1 coffee (lowercase)
   - 'movie' (watching movie): 1 coffee (lowercase)
   - Any other event: ignore
3. Coffee requirement depends on case:
   - Lowercase events: 1 coffee
   - Uppercase events: 2 coffees
4. If total coffee exceeds 3, return "You need extra sleep"

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Identify the events that count
- We need to check if each event is one of: 'cw', 'dog', 'cat', 'movie'
- Any other event should be ignored

### Step 2: Determine coffee based on case
- If the event is lowercase: 1 coffee
- If the event is uppercase: 2 coffees

### Step 3: Sum all coffee requirements
- Initialize a counter to 0
- For each event in the input list:
  - Check if it's one of the valid events
  - If valid, add 1 or 2 to the counter based on case
  - If not valid, skip it

### Step 4: Return result
- If total coffee > 3: return "You need extra sleep"
- Otherwise: return the total coffee count

```python
def how_much_coffee(events):
    coffee_count = 0
    
    for event in events:
        # Convert event to lowercase to check for valid events
        event_lower = event.lower()
        
        # Check if the event is one of the valid types
        if event_lower == 'cw' or event_lower == 'dog' or event_lower == 'cat' or event_lower == 'movie':
            # If original event is uppercase, need 2 coffees, otherwise 1 coffee
            if event.isupper():
                coffee_count += 2
            else:
                coffee_count += 1
    
    # Check if coffee exceeds 3
    if coffee_count > 3:
        return "You need extra sleep"
    
    return coffee_count
```

1. Iterate through each event in the input list
2. Convert each event to lowercase to check if it matches any valid event type

4. After processing all events, check if the total exceeds 3

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the event types and their coffee requirements
- 'cw', 'dog', 'cat', 'movie': these are specific events that always need 1 coffee each

Wait, I need to re-read. The problem says:
- For specific events ('cw', 'dog', 'cat', 'movie') → 1 coffee if lowercase, 2 coffees if uppercase
- Other events → ignore

Actually, let me re-read carefully:
> If it is downcase/lowercase you need 1 coffee by events and if it is uppercase you need 2 coffees.

This applies to all events, not just specific ones. But the specific events are listed, and others are ignored.

So the rule is:
1. For any event string:
   - If it's one of the specific events ('cw', 'dog', 'cat', 'movie'): count it
   - If it's any other string: ignore it
   - For events that are counted: if lowercase → 1 coffee, if uppercase → 2 coffees

### Step 2: Algorithm
1. Initialize coffee counter to 0
2. For each event in the input list:
   - Check if event is one of: 'cw', 'dog', 'cat', 'movie'
   - If yes: 
     - If the event is in lowercase → add 1 coffee
     - If the event is in uppercase → add 2 coffees
   - If no: ignore (add 0)
3. If total coffee > 3: return "You need extra sleep"
4. Otherwise: return total coffee count

### Step 3: Implementation

```python
def how_much_coffee(events):
    coffee_count = 0
    
    for event in events:
        # Check if event is one of the specific events
        if event in ['cw', 'dog', 'cat', 'movie']:
            # Check case: lowercase = 1 coffee, uppercase = 2 coffees
            if event.islower():
                coffee_count += 1
            else:
                coffee_count += 2
    
    # Check if we need extra sleep
    if coffee_count > 3:
        return "You need extra sleep"
    
    return coffee_count
```

Wait, I need to be careful about the case sensitivity. The problem says "each event can be downcase/lowercase, or uppercase". This applies to all events, not just the specific ones.

But actually, for non-specific events, they are ignored. So only specific events contribute to coffee count.

For example: 
- 'Cw' → uppercase → 2 coffees
- 'cw' → lowercase → 1 coffee  
- 'DOG' → uppercase → 2 coffees (but 'dog' is a specific event, so it would be counted)
- 'fish' → not a specific event → ignored

So my solution above is correct.

- 'cw': lowercase → 1 coffee
- 'DOG': uppercase → 2 coffees (since 'dog' is a specific event)
- 'movie': lowercase → 1 coffee
- Total: 1 + 2 + 1 = 4 → return "You need extra sleep"

Another example:
- events = ['cw', 'dog']
- 'cw': lowercase → 1 coffee
- 'dog': lowercase → 1 coffee
- Total: 2 → return 2

  - Uppercase: 2 coffees

- Empty input list
- All uppercase events
- All lowercase events
- Mixed case events

```python
    coffee_count = 0
    
    for event in events:
        # Convert to lowercase for comparison to avoid case sensitivity issues
        event_lower = event.lower()
        
        # Check if event is one of the valid types
        if event_lower == "cw" or event_lower == "dog" or event_lower == "cat" or event_lower == "movie":
```
